<a id='question-128'></a>

### Question 128: Left Coronary Artery (LCA) Origin from Left Aortic Sinus: Left Anterior Descending (LAD) & Left Circumflex (LCx)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the normal origin of the left coronary artery (LCA) from the left aortic sinus, its bifurcation into the left anterior descending (LAD) and left circumflex (LCx) arteries, and the clinical consequences of anomalous origins or occlusions, specifically related to myocardial infarction patterns. (理解左冠状动脉从主动脉左冠窦起源的正常情况，其分支为左前降支和左环绕支，以及异常起源或闭塞的临床后果，特别是与心肌梗塞模式相关的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing chest pain radiating to his left arm and jaw, which started 2 hours ago. He is diaphoretic and nauseous. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg and a heart rate of 110 bpm. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows ST-segment elevation in leads II, III, and aVF, along with reciprocal ST-segment depression in leads I and aVL. Cardiac biomarkers (troponin I) are elevated. Coronary angiography reveals a complete occlusion of the proximal right coronary artery (RCA). The patient undergoes emergent percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) with stent placement in the RCA. Post-procedure, the patient's chest pain resolves, and his ECG changes normalize. However, the cardiologist notes an unusual finding during the angiography: the left coronary artery originates from the left aortic sinus, bifurcates into the left anterior descending (LAD) and left circumflex (LCx) arteries, and courses normally. The LAD supplies the anterior wall, septum, and apex, while the LCx supplies the lateral and posterior walls. The right coronary artery (RCA) supplies the inferior wall and the right ventricle. The occlusion was confirmed to be in the proximal RCA, explaining the inferior wall ST-elevation myocardial infarction (STEMI). The question asks which artery, originating from the left aortic sinus, supplies the anterior wall and septum of the left ventricle.

(A) Right Coronary Artery (RCA)
(B) Left Circumflex Artery (LCx)
(C) Left Anterior Descending Artery (LAD)
(D) Posterior Descending Artery (PDA)
(E) Marginal Artery

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient presents with classic symptoms of acute myocardial infarction (MI): crushing chest pain, radiation, diaphoresis, nausea.
- ECG shows ST elevation in inferior leads (II, III, aVF) and reciprocal depression in lateral leads (I, aVL), indicating an inferior wall STEMI.
- Cardiac biomarkers are elevated, confirming myocardial necrosis.
- Coronary angiography confirms occlusion of the proximal RCA, the typical culprit artery for inferior wall MI.
- The vignette explicitly describes the normal origin of the LCA from the left aortic sinus and its bifurcation into LAD and LCx.
- The question asks which artery, originating from the left aortic sinus (as part of the LCA), supplies the anterior wall and septum. This requires identifying the specific branch of the LCA responsible for this territory.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Coronary Artery Anatomy:** The aorta has three aortic sinuses (or sinuses of Valsalva) just above the aortic valve: right, left, and non-coronary (posterior).
    - The right coronary artery (RCA) typically originates from the right aortic sinus.
    - The left coronary artery (LCA) typically originates from the left aortic sinus.
    - The left coronary artery (LCA) almost immediately bifurcates into two main branches:
        - **Left Anterior Descending Artery (LAD):** Also known as the anterior interventricular artery. It runs in the anterior interventricular groove towards the apex of the heart. It supplies the anterior wall of the left ventricle, the anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum, and the apex.
        - **Left Circumflex Artery (LCx):** It runs in the atrioventricular groove to the left of the heart. It supplies the lateral wall of the left ventricle and the left atrium. In about 15% of individuals (left dominant circulation), the LCx gives rise to the posterior descending artery (PDA), which supplies the inferior wall and posterior septum. In about 85% (right dominant circulation), the PDA arises from the RCA.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's inferior wall MI is caused by the RCA occlusion. The question specifically asks about the artery originating from the left aortic sinus that supplies the anterior wall and septum. Based on the standard anatomy described in the vignette, this is the LAD.
- **Embryology:** The coronary arteries develop from endothelial progenitor cells originating from the aortic arches. The LCA and RCA develop from the truncus arteriosus. The specific branching patterns are established during development. Anomalous origins (e.g., LCA from the right aortic sinus) can occur but are less common and often associated with specific clinical syndromes. The vignette confirms the normal origin from the left aortic sinus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Right Coronary Artery (RCA): The RCA originates from the *right* aortic sinus, not the left. It primarily supplies the right ventricle, the inferior wall of the left ventricle (in right-dominant circulation), the SA node (in ~60% of people), and the AV node (in ~90% of people). It does *not* supply the anterior wall or septum. Incorrect origin and territory.
- (B) Left Circumflex Artery (LCx): The LCx originates from the left aortic sinus as a branch of the LCA, but it primarily supplies the lateral wall of the left ventricle and the left atrium. It does *not* supply the anterior wall or septum. Incorrect territory.
- (D) Posterior Descending Artery (PDA): The PDA supplies the inferior wall and posterior third of the interventricular septum. Its origin varies (usually RCA, sometimes LCx). It originates from either the RCA or LCx, not directly from the LCA origin point, and it does not supply the anterior wall or septum. Incorrect territory and origin.
- (E) Marginal Artery: Marginal arteries are branches of the LCx (left marginal) or RCA (right marginal) that run along the margins of the heart. They supply the respective lateral walls. They do not supply the anterior wall or septum and are branches, not the main vessel originating from the LCA bifurcation. Incorrect territory and level.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Left Anterior Descending (LAD) | Left Circumflex (LCx) | Right Coronary Artery (RCA) | Posterior Descending (PDA) | Marginal Artery |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------|
| **Origin**           | Left Aortic Sinus (from LCA)    | Left Aortic Sinus (from LCA) | Right Aortic Sinus          | RCA or LCx                  | LCx or RCA      |
| **Main Territory**   | Anterior LV wall, Septum, Apex   | Lateral LV wall, LA      | Inferior LV wall, RV, SA/AV nodes | Inferior LV wall, Post. Septum | Lateral LV wall |
| **Key Landmark**     | Anterior Interventricular Groove | Atrioventricular Groove | Right Atrioventricular Groove | Posterior Interventricular Groove | Heart Margin    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The left coronary artery originates from the left aortic sinus and bifurcates into the LAD and LCx. The LAD supplies the anterior wall and septum, while the LCx supplies the lateral wall. The RCA originates from the right aortic sinus and typically supplies the inferior wall. Knowing the origin and territories of the coronary arteries is crucial for understanding myocardial infarction patterns. (了解冠状动脉的起源和供应区域至关重要，这有助于理解心肌梗塞的模式。)
Differential: Distinguish LAD (anterior wall/septum) from LCx (lateral wall) and RCA (inferior wall) territories. Remember the origin of each major artery from the aortic sinuses.

<a id='question-129'></a>

### Question 129: Myocardial Infarction & LAD Branches
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical distribution of the Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery branches (septal perforators and diagonal branches) and their clinical correlation with myocardial infarction symptoms and ECG changes. (理解左前降动脉分支（隔中穿支和斜支）的解剖分布及其与心肌梗死症状和心电图变化的临床相关性)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing substernal chest pain radiating to his left arm and jaw, which started 2 hours ago. He is diaphoretic and nauseous. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg and a heart rate of 110 bpm. Physical examination reveals an S4 gallop. An emergency ECG shows ST-segment elevation in leads V1-V4, reciprocal ST-segment depression in leads II, III, and aVF, and new onset left bundle branch block (LBBB). Cardiac biomarkers (troponin I) are elevated. Coronary angiography reveals a complete occlusion of the proximal Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery.

Which of the following myocardial regions is MOST likely affected by this occlusion?

(A) Right Ventricle
(B) Inferior Wall of the Left Ventricle
(C) Anterolateral Wall of the Left Ventricle
(D) Posterior Wall of the Left Ventricle
(E) Interventricular Septum (Posterior 1/3)

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, risk factors (HTN, hyperlipidemia), acute onset severe crushing chest pain radiating to left arm/jaw, diaphoresis, nausea. Classic symptoms of acute myocardial infarction (AMI).
- **Vitals:** Hypotension (90/60 mmHg) and tachycardia (110 bpm) suggest cardiogenic shock or significant myocardial dysfunction.
- **Physical Exam:** S4 gallop indicates decreased left ventricular compliance, often seen in hypertension or ischemia.
- **ECG Findings:** ST-segment elevation in V1-V4 indicates injury to the anterior wall of the left ventricle. Reciprocal ST depression in inferior leads (II, III, aVF) further supports anterior wall involvement. New LBBB can occur with extensive anterior MI, often involving the conduction system supplied by the LAD.
- **Cardiac Biomarkers:** Elevated troponin I confirms myocardial necrosis.
- **Coronary Angiography:** Complete occlusion of the proximal LAD artery is the definitive finding.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the myocardial region affected by the proximal LAD occlusion.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- The Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery, also known as the anterior interventricular artery, is the first major branch of the left coronary artery.
- It runs in the anterior interventricular groove towards the apex of the heart.
- **Branches:**
    - **Septal Perforators:** Arise from the LAD and penetrate the interventricular septum, supplying the anterior two-thirds (2/3) of the interventricular septum.
    - **Diagonal Branches:** Arise from the LAD and run obliquely across the surface of the left ventricle towards the apex, supplying the anterolateral wall of the left ventricle.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Occlusion of the proximal LAD artery typically affects the areas supplied by both its septal perforators and its diagonal branches. This includes the anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum and the anterior and anterolateral walls of the left ventricle.
- **ECG Correlation:** The ECG findings (ST elevation in V1-V4) directly correlate with injury to the anterior wall of the left ventricle, which is supplied by the LAD. The new LBBB can also be associated with LAD occlusion, as the LAD often supplies the left bundle branch.
- **Therefore:** The anterolateral wall of the left ventricle is a major region supplied by the diagonal branches of the LAD, making it highly susceptible to infarction following proximal LAD occlusion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Right Ventricle: The right ventricle is primarily supplied by the right coronary artery (RCA) in most individuals (right dominance) or by the circumflex artery (LCx) in left dominance. LAD occlusion typically does not affect the right ventricle.
- (B) Inferior Wall of the Left Ventricle: The inferior wall of the left ventricle is primarily supplied by the posterior descending artery (PDA), which usually arises from the RCA (right dominance) or the LCx (left dominance). LAD occlusion does not typically affect the inferior wall.
- (D) Posterior Wall of the Left Ventricle: The posterior wall of the left ventricle is primarily supplied by the PDA (usually from RCA) and the marginal branches of the LCx. LAD occlusion does not typically affect the posterior wall.
- (E) Interventricular Septum (Posterior 1/3): The posterior one-third (1/3) of the interventricular septum is typically supplied by the posterior descending artery (PDA), which usually arises from the RCA. The LAD supplies the anterior two-thirds (2/3) of the septum via septal perforators.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | LAD Supply                                  | RCA Supply                                     | LCx Supply                                     |
| :---------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| Anterior LV Wall              | Yes (Diagonal Branches)                     | No                                             | No                                             |
| Anterolateral LV Wall         | Yes (Diagonal Branches)                     | No                                             | No                                             |
| Anterior 2/3 IVS              | Yes (Septal Perforators)                    | No                                             | No                                             |
| Inferior LV Wall              | No                                             | Yes (via PDA)                                  | Yes (via PDA if RCA is left dominant)           |
| Posterior LV Wall             | No                                             | Yes (via PDA, marginal branches)               | Yes (marginal branches)                         |
| Posterior 1/3 IVS             | No                                             | Yes (via PDA)                                  | No                                             |
| Right Ventricle               | No                                             | Yes (major supply)                             | Minor supply (sometimes)                       |
| SA Node                       | Usually RCA (60%), sometimes LCx (40%)      | Most common (60%)                             | Less common (40%)                             |
| AV Node                       | Usually RCA (85-90%)                        | Most common (85-90%)                          | Less common (10-15%)                          |
| Bundle Branches/Purkinje Fibers | LAD (LBBB), RCA (RBBB), LCx (variable) | RCA (RBBB), LAD (LBBB), LCx (variable)          | LAD (LBBB), RCA (RBBB), LCx (variable)          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The proximal LAD artery supplies the anterior 2/3 of the interventricular septum (via septal perforators) and the anterior/anterolateral walls of the left ventricle (via diagonal branches). Occlusion typically causes anterior MI (ST elevation in V1-V4) and can lead to LBBB. Differentiating LAD supply from RCA (inferior/posterior wall, RV) and LCx (lateral/posterior wall) supply is crucial for localizing MI based on ECG findings and clinical presentation.

<a id='question-130'></a>

### Question 130: Coronary Artery Anatomy & Myocardial Infarction
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the branching pattern of the Left Circumflex (LCx) artery, specifically the obtuse marginal (OM) branches supplying the lateral wall of the left ventricle, and the clinical consequences of occlusion. (理解左旋支动脉的分支模式，特别是供应左心室侧壁的钝缘支，以及闭塞的临床后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing substernal chest pain radiating to his left arm and jaw, which started 2 hours ago. He is diaphoretic and nauseated. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg and a heart rate of 110 bpm. An initial ECG reveals ST-segment elevation in leads I, aVL, and V5-V6. Cardiac troponin levels are markedly elevated. Coronary angiography is performed, revealing a complete occlusion of the proximal Left Circumflex (LCx) artery. The LCx artery gives off two prominent obtuse marginal (OM) branches before the occlusion. Which region of the left ventricle is MOST likely affected by this occlusion, leading to the observed ECG changes?

(A) Anterior wall
(B) Inferior wall
(C) Apical wall
(D) Lateral wall
(E) Posterior wall

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62M, HTN, HLD, acute crushing chest pain radiating to left arm/jaw, diaphoresis, nausea. Classic symptoms of acute myocardial infarction (AMI).
- **Vitals:** Hypotension (90/60 mmHg) and tachycardia (110 bpm) suggest cardiogenic shock or significant myocardial dysfunction.
- **ECG:** ST-segment elevation in I, aVL, V5-V6. These leads reflect the electrical activity of the lateral wall of the left ventricle.
- **Cardiac Troponin:** Elevated levels confirm myocardial necrosis.
- **Coronary Angiography:** Complete occlusion of the proximal LCx artery. The LCx artery is a major branch of the left main coronary artery.
- **LCx Branches:** The LCx gives off two prominent obtuse marginal (OM) branches *before* the occlusion.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which region of the left ventricle is affected by the occlusion of the proximal LCx, given the ECG findings and the branching pattern.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Left Circumflex (LCx) Artery:** Originates from the left main coronary artery. It courses in the atrioventricular groove (coronary sulcus) towards the posterior aspect of the heart.
- **Obtuse Marginal (OM) Branches:** These are typically the first major branches off the LCx artery. They run along the obtuse margin of the heart (the border between the left ventricle and the left atrium) and supply the lateral wall of the left ventricle. There are usually one or two major OM branches.
- **Supply Territory:** The LCx artery and its OM branches primarily supply the lateral wall of the left ventricle, the left atrium, and the posterior one-third of the interventricular septum (often supplied by the posterior descending artery, which can arise from either the LCx or the right coronary artery - RCA).
- **ECG Correlation:**
    - Leads I and aVL reflect the high lateral wall (near the apex).
    - Leads V5 and V6 reflect the low lateral wall (near the base).
    - ST elevation in I, aVL, V5, V6 indicates injury/infarction to the lateral wall of the left ventricle.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Occlusion of the proximal LCx, especially before the origin of the OM branches, will lead to ischemia and infarction of the territory supplied by these branches, namely the lateral wall of the left ventricle. This corresponds directly to the ECG findings (ST elevation in I, aVL, V5-V6).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Anterior wall: Supplied primarily by the Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery, a branch of the left main coronary artery. LAD occlusion typically causes ST elevation in leads V1-V4 (septal) and V1-V6 (anterior). Incorrect.
- (B) Inferior wall: Supplied primarily by the Right Coronary Artery (RCA) in most individuals (right dominance) or by the LCx artery in left dominance. RCA occlusion typically causes ST elevation in leads II, III, aVF. Incorrect.
- (C) Apical wall: Supplied by the LAD artery, particularly its septal and diagonal branches. Apical infarcts often present with ST elevation in V1-V2, sometimes with reciprocal ST depression in inferior leads. Incorrect.
- (E) Posterior wall: Supplied by the Posterior Descending Artery (PDA), which usually arises from the RCA (right dominance) but can arise from the LCx (left dominance). Posterior wall infarcts often cause ST depression in V1-V3 and tall R waves in V1-V2, sometimes with ST elevation in posterior leads (V7-V9). While the LCx can supply the posterior wall (if it gives rise to the PDA), the *proximal* occlusion described here, *before* the OM branches, and the specific ECG findings (I, aVL, V5-V6) point strongly to lateral wall involvement by the OM branches. The question specifies the occlusion is proximal to the OM branches, making lateral wall ischemia the most direct and likely consequence. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure        | Artery Supply                                  | ECG Leads (ST Elevation) | Clinical Presentation                                  |
|-----------------|------------------------------------------------|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| Anterior Wall    | LAD                                            | V1-V4 (Septal), V1-V6 (Anterior) | Chest pain, possible cardiogenic shock                 |
| Inferior Wall    | RCA (usually), LCx (left dominance)             | II, III, aVF              | Chest pain, possible bradycardia/heart block           |
| Apical Wall      | LAD (Septal, Diagonal branches)                | V1-V2                    | Chest pain, possible apical aneurysm                   |
| **Lateral Wall** | **LCx (OM branches)**                          | **I, aVL, V5-V6**         | **Chest pain, possible heart failure**                |
| Posterior Wall   | PDA (RCA usually, LCx sometimes)               | V1-V3 (ST depression), V7-V9 (ST elevation) | Chest pain, often subtle ECG changes, possible HF    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The Left Circumflex (LCx) artery and its obtuse marginal (OM) branches are the primary blood supply to the lateral wall of the left ventricle. Occlusion of the proximal LCx, especially before the OM branches, typically results in a lateral wall myocardial infarction, characterized by ST-segment elevation in ECG leads I, aVL, V5, and V6. Differentiating LCx territory infarcts from LAD (anterior/septal/apical) and RCA (inferior/posterior) territory infarcts relies heavily on ECG localization.

<a id='question-131'></a>

### Question 131: Coronary Dominance Determination
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identification of coronary artery dominance based on the origin of the posterior descending artery (PDA) and its clinical implications, specifically relating to myocardial infarction patterns. (确定冠状动脉优势侧的依据：后行间支动脉的起源及其临床意义，特别是心肌梗死模式。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing substernal chest pain radiating to his left arm and jaw, which started 2 hours ago. He is diaphoretic and nauseated. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg and a heart rate of 110 bpm. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows ST-segment elevation in leads II, III, and aVF, along with reciprocal ST-segment depression in leads I and aVL. Cardiac catheterization reveals a complete occlusion of the right coronary artery (RCA) proximal to its marginal branches. Angiography also demonstrates that the posterior descending artery (PDA) arises from the RCA, and the posterior left ventricular artery (PLVA) arises from the circumflex artery. Given this finding, which of the following myocardial regions is MOST likely to be affected by this occlusion, in addition to the inferior wall?

(A) Anterior wall and apex
(B) Lateral wall
(C) Septal wall
(D) Posterior wall
(E) High lateral wall

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62M, HTN, HLD, acute crushing chest pain radiating to left arm/jaw, diaphoresis, nausea. Classic symptoms of acute myocardial infarction (AMI).
- **Vitals:** Hypotension (90/60 mmHg) and tachycardia (110 bpm) suggest cardiogenic shock or significant myocardial dysfunction.
- **ECG:** ST elevation in II, III, aVF indicates inferior wall MI. Reciprocal changes in I, aVL confirm this.
- **Cardiac Catheterization:** Complete occlusion of the proximal RCA. This explains the inferior wall MI (supplied by RCA in most people).
- **Angiography Finding:** PDA arises from the RCA, and PLVA arises from the circumflex artery. This defines the coronary circulation pattern as **right dominant**.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which *additional* region is likely affected by the RCA occlusion, given the right-dominant pattern.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Coronary Dominance:** Refers to which coronary artery (RCA or LCx) gives rise to the PDA.
    - **Right Dominant (85%):** RCA gives rise to the PDA. The RCA typically supplies the right atrium, right ventricle, sinoatrial (SA) node (in ~60%), atrioventricular (AV) node (in ~90%), inferior wall of the left ventricle, and the posterior interventricular septum via the PDA.
    - **Left Dominant (10%):** Left Circumflex artery (LCx) gives rise to the PDA. The LCx supplies the left atrium, lateral wall of the left ventricle, and the posterior interventricular septum via the PDA.
    - **Codominant (5%):** Both RCA and LCx contribute branches that form the PDA.
- **RCA Occlusion:** In a right-dominant system, occlusion of the proximal RCA affects:
    - Inferior wall of the left ventricle (supplied by RCA marginal branches).
    - Right ventricle (supplied by RCA).
    - SA node (in ~60%, supplied by RCA).
    - AV node (in ~90%, supplied by RCA).
    - Posterior interventricular septum (supplied by the PDA originating from the RCA).
    - Posterior wall of the left ventricle (supplied by the PDA originating from the RCA).
- **Clinical Correlation:** The ECG findings (ST elevation in II, III, aVF) confirm an inferior wall MI, consistent with RCA occlusion. The question asks for the *additional* region affected. Since the PDA originates from the RCA in this right-dominant system, the posterior wall of the left ventricle, which is supplied by the PDA, is also at high risk of infarction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Anterior wall and apex: Typically supplied by the left anterior descending (LAD) artery, which arises from the left main coronary artery. RCA occlusion does not directly affect this region.
- (B) Lateral wall: Primarily supplied by the LCx (and sometimes LAD). RCA occlusion does not directly affect the main lateral wall.
- (C) Septal wall: The anterior 2/3 of the interventricular septum is supplied by the LAD. The posterior 1/3 is supplied by the PDA. While the posterior 1/3 *is* affected in this case, the question asks for a *region*, and the posterior wall is a more distinct and commonly recognized region affected by PDA occlusion. Furthermore, the septal involvement is part of the posterior infarct territory.
- (D) Posterior wall: Supplied by the PDA (in right dominance) and the PLVA (from LCx). Since the PDA originates from the RCA in this patient, occlusion of the RCA will lead to infarction of the posterior wall supplied by the PDA. This is the correct additional region.
- (E) High lateral wall: Supplied by the proximal LCx. RCA occlusion does not directly affect this region.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Right Dominant (RCA -> PDA) | Left Dominant (LCx -> PDA) | Codominant (RCA & LCx -> PDA) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|----------------------------|------------------------------|
| PDA Origin           | RCA                         | LCx                        | Both RCA & LCx               |
| Posterior Wall Supply| RCA via PDA                 | LCx via PDA                | RCA & LCx via PDA             |
| Inferior Wall Supply | RCA                         | RCA                        | RCA                          |
| Lateral Wall Supply  | LCx                         | LCx                        | LCx                          |
| Septal Supply (Post) | RCA via PDA                 | LCx via PDA                | RCA & LCx via PDA             |
| Clinical Scenario    | Proximal RCA occlusion affects Inferior + Posterior walls | Proximal LCx occlusion affects Lateral + Posterior walls | Proximal RCA occlusion affects Inferior + Posterior walls; Proximal LCx occlusion affects Lateral + Posterior walls |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Coronary dominance determines the blood supply to the posterior myocardium. In right dominance (most common), the RCA gives rise to the PDA, supplying the posterior interventricular septum and posterior left ventricular wall. Therefore, proximal RCA occlusion typically causes inferior and posterior wall MIs. Differentiating coronary dominance is crucial for predicting the extent of myocardial damage in cases of coronary artery occlusion. Key differentials include LAD occlusion (anterior/septal/apical MI) and LCx occlusion (lateral/posterior MI in left dominance).

<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: Myocardial Infarction Territories: Anterior Wall (LAD: V1-V4)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical distribution of the left anterior descending (LAD) coronary artery and its clinical correlation with myocardial infarction affecting the anterior wall of the left ventricle, specifically the septum and apex. (理解左前降支冠状动脉的解剖分布及其与左心室前壁心肌梗死的相关性，特别是间隔和尖端。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing substernal chest pain radiating to his left arm, which started 2 hours ago. He is diaphoretic and nauseous. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg and a heart rate of 110 bpm. An electrocardiogram (ECG) reveals ST-segment elevation in leads V1, V2, V3, and V4, along with reciprocal ST-segment depression in leads II, III, and aVF. Cardiac enzymes (troponin I) are elevated. Echocardiography shows akinesis of the anterior wall and apical segment of the left ventricle, with preserved septal motion. Which coronary artery is most likely occluded, leading to this clinical presentation?

(A) Right coronary artery (RCA)
(B) Left circumflex artery (LCx)
(C) Left anterior descending artery (LAD)
(D) Left main coronary artery (LMCA)
(E) Posterior descending artery (PDA)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia), acute onset severe crushing chest pain radiating to the left arm, diaphoresis, nausea. This strongly suggests an acute myocardial infarction (AMI).
- **Vitals:** Hypotension (90/60 mmHg) and tachycardia (110 bpm) can occur in AMI, especially with significant myocardial damage affecting pump function.
- **ECG Findings:** ST-segment elevation in V1-V4 is the most critical clue. These leads view the anterior and septal aspects of the left ventricle. ST elevation indicates transmural ischemia/infarction in the territory supplied by the artery corresponding to these leads. Reciprocal changes (ST depression in inferior leads II, III, aVF) further support an anterior wall MI.
- **Cardiac Enzymes:** Elevated troponin I confirms myocardial necrosis.
- **Echocardiography:** Akinesis (lack of movement) of the anterior wall and apical segment of the left ventricle directly correlates with the ECG findings and points to ischemia/infarction in the territory supplied by the LAD. Preserved septal motion is somewhat atypical for a complete LAD occlusion but can occur with proximal LAD occlusion sparing septal perforators or with collateral circulation. However, the primary affected area described (anterior wall, apex) strongly implicates the LAD.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the occluded coronary artery responsible for this specific pattern of infarction.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Coronary Artery Anatomy:** The left coronary artery arises from the left aortic sinus and bifurcates into the LAD and LCx. The LAD typically runs in the anterior interventricular groove, supplying the anterior wall of the left ventricle, the anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum, and the apex. The LCx runs in the atrioventricular groove, supplying the lateral and posterior walls of the left ventricle and the left atrium. The RCA typically supplies the right ventricle, the inferior wall of the left ventricle, the posterior one-third of the interventricular septum (via the PDA in right-dominant circulation), and the sinoatrial (SA) node in most individuals.
- **Myocardial Infarction Territories:**
    - **LAD Occlusion:** Causes anterior wall MI (V1-V4), anteroseptal MI (V1-V2), or extensive anterior MI involving apex (V3-V4). Can lead to cardiogenic shock due to large muscle mass affected.
    - **LCx Occlusion:** Causes lateral wall MI (I, aVL, V5-V6) or posterior wall MI (reciprocal changes in V1-V3, ST elevation in posterior leads V7-V9).
    - **RCA Occlusion:** Causes inferior wall MI (II, III, aVF), posterior wall MI, or right ventricular MI (V4R). Can cause bradycardia or heart block if the SA or AV node is affected.
    - **LMCA Occlusion:** Catastrophic, involves both LAD and LCx territories, leading to extensive anterior, lateral, and septal infarction. Often fatal.
    - **PDA Occlusion:** Supplies the posterior septum and posterior wall. Occlusion causes posterior or posteroseptal MI.
- **Correlation:** The ECG findings (ST elevation in V1-V4) and echocardiographic findings (akinesis of anterior wall and apex) are classic for an occlusion of the LAD coronary artery.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Right coronary artery (RCA):** Occlusion typically causes inferior wall MI (ST elevation in II, III, aVF) and potentially posterior wall MI or right ventricular MI. This contradicts the ECG findings in V1-V4.
- (B) **Left circumflex artery (LCx):** Occlusion typically causes lateral wall MI (ST elevation in I, aVL, V5-V6) or posterior wall MI (ST depression in V1-V3, ST elevation in V7-V9). This contradicts the ECG findings in V1-V4.
- (D) **Left main coronary artery (LMCA):** Occlusion affects both LAD and LCx territories, leading to massive anterior, lateral, and septal infarction. ECG would show widespread ST elevation (e.g., V1-V6, I, aVL) and often profound hemodynamic instability. While severe, the specific pattern (V1-V4) points more directly to the LAD.
- (E) **Posterior descending artery (PDA):** The PDA is usually a branch of the RCA (right dominance, ~85%) or LCx (left dominance, ~15%). It supplies the posterior septum and posterior wall. Occlusion causes posterior MI (ST elevation in V7-V9, reciprocal changes in V1-V3). This contradicts the ECG findings in V1-V4.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | LAD Occlusion (Correct) | RCA Occlusion | LCx Occlusion | LMCA Occlusion | PDA Occlusion |
|----------------------|-------------------------|---------------|---------------|----------------|---------------|
| **ECG Leads**        | V1-V4 (Anterior/Septal) | II, III, aVF (Inferior) | I, aVL, V5-V6 (Lateral) | Widespread (Anterior, Lateral, Septal) | V7-V9 (Posterior), Reciprocal V1-V3 |
| **Affected Wall(s)** | Anterior, Septal, Apex  | Inferior, Posterior, RV | Lateral, Posterior | Anterior, Lateral, Septal | Posterior, Septal |
| **Typical Location** | Anterior Interventricular Groove | Right AV Groove, Posterior AV Groove | Left AV Groove | Left Main Stem | Posterior AV Groove |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The ECG pattern of ST elevation in leads V1-V4 is pathognomonic for an anterior wall myocardial infarction, typically caused by occlusion of the left anterior descending (LAD) coronary artery. Differentiating MI locations based on ECG leads is crucial for diagnosis and management. Remember that inferior MIs involve leads II, III, aVF (RCA), lateral MIs involve leads I, aVL, V5-V6 (LCx), and posterior MIs involve reciprocal changes in V1-V3 and ST elevation in posterior leads (RCA or LCx depending on dominance).

<a id='question-133'></a>

### Question 133: Myocardial Infarction Territories
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of myocardial infarction based on ECG changes and clinical presentation, specifically involving the anterolateral wall supplied by the Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery or Left Circumflex (LCx) artery. (根据心电图变化和临床表现定位心肌梗死，特别是由左前降支 (LAD) 或左旋支 (LCx) 供血的内侧壁梗死。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing chest pain radiating to his left arm and jaw, which started 2 hours ago. He is diaphoretic and nauseated. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg and a heart rate of 110 bpm. Physical examination reveals an S4 gallop. An emergent 12-lead ECG shows ST-segment elevation in leads I, aVL, V4, V5, and V6, with reciprocal ST-segment depression in leads II, III, and aVF. Troponin levels are markedly elevated. Coronary angiography reveals a complete occlusion of the proximal Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery.

Which of the following anatomical structures is MOST likely affected by this myocardial infarction?

(A) Right Ventricle
(B) Inferior Wall of the Left Ventricle
(C) Apex of the Left Ventricle
(D) Anterolateral Wall of the Left Ventricle
(E) Interventricular Septum (posterior portion)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, risk factors (HTN, hyperlipidemia), acute severe crushing chest pain radiating to left arm/jaw, diaphoresis, nausea. Classic symptoms of acute myocardial infarction (AMI).
- **Vitals:** Hypotension (90/60 mmHg) and tachycardia (110 bpm) suggest cardiogenic shock or significant myocardial dysfunction.
- **Physical Exam:** S4 gallop indicates decreased left ventricular compliance, often seen in hypertension or ischemia.
- **ECG Findings:** ST-segment elevation in I, aVL, V4, V5, V6 indicates acute injury. Reciprocal ST depression in II, III, aVF confirms the location.
- **ECG Localization:**
    - Leads I and aVL reflect the high lateral wall of the left ventricle.
    - Leads V4, V5, and V6 reflect the anterolateral wall of the left ventricle.
    - The combination points strongly to an anterolateral MI.
- **Coronary Angiography:** Occlusion of the proximal LAD artery confirms the source of the infarction. The LAD typically supplies the anterior wall, apex, anterior 2/3 of the interventricular septum, and the anterolateral papillary muscle. However, in some individuals (left dominant circulation), the LCx supplies the lateral wall (I, aVL, V5, V6). Given the ECG findings (I, aVL, V4-V6), the affected area is the anterolateral wall.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical structure affected by the MI, based on the clinical and diagnostic findings.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **LAD Artery Supply:** The Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery, a branch of the left coronary artery, typically supplies the anterior wall of the left ventricle, the apex, the anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum, and the anterolateral papillary muscle.
- **LCx Artery Supply:** The Left Circumflex (LCx) artery, also a branch of the left coronary artery, typically supplies the lateral wall of the left ventricle and the posterior wall (in right-dominant circulation, it supplies the posterior descending artery (PDA) and the posterior wall). In left-dominant circulation (~15-20% of people), the LCx supplies the PDA and the posterior wall, and the marginal branches supply the lateral wall.
- **ECG Correlation:**
    - Leads V1-V4: Anterior wall (LAD)
    - Leads I, aVL, V5, V6: Lateral wall (LCx or LAD depending on dominance)
    - Leads II, III, aVF: Inferior wall (Right Coronary Artery - RCA or LCx)
    - Leads V1-V2 (ST depression, tall R waves): Posterior wall (RCA or LCx)
- **Clinical Findings:** The ECG shows ST elevation in I, aVL, V4, V5, V6. This pattern indicates injury to the anterolateral wall of the left ventricle. The occlusion of the proximal LAD artery is consistent with this finding, as the LAD supplies this region. Even if the circulation were left-dominant, the ECG pattern points to the anterolateral wall.
- **Anterolateral Wall:** This region is located on the anterior and lateral aspects of the left ventricle, typically corresponding to ECG leads I, aVL, V5, and V6. The anterior portion (V4-V6) is supplied by the LAD, while the high lateral portion (I, aVL) can be supplied by either the LAD or the LCx depending on coronary dominance. The combination of V4-V6 and I/aVL strongly implicates the anterolateral wall.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Right Ventricle: MI involving the right ventricle typically presents with ST elevation in right-sided leads (V4R) and inferior leads (II, III, aVF). This patient's ECG does not show these changes. Right ventricular infarction is usually associated with inferior wall MI (RCA occlusion).
- (B) Inferior Wall of the Left Ventricle: MI involving the inferior wall presents with ST elevation in leads II, III, and aVF, often with reciprocal ST depression in I and aVL. This patient has reciprocal changes in II, III, aVF but ST elevation in I, aVL, V4-V6, ruling out an isolated inferior MI. Inferior wall is typically supplied by the RCA or LCx.
- (C) Apex of the Left Ventricle: MI involving the apex typically presents with ST elevation in leads V1-V4. While the LAD supplies the apex, the ECG findings in this case (I, aVL, V4-V6) are not characteristic of an apical MI.
- (E) Interventricular Septum (posterior portion): MI involving the posterior septum typically presents with ST depression in V1-V3 and tall R waves, sometimes with ST elevation in posterior leads (V7-V9). This patient's ECG does not show these changes. The posterior septum is typically supplied by the PDA, which arises from the RCA in right-dominant circulation or the LCx in left-dominant circulation. The anterior 2/3 of the septum is supplied by the LAD.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | ECG Leads Affected (ST Elevation) | Typical Artery | Clinical Presentation Clues                                  |
| :------------------------ | :------------------------------- | :------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- |
| Anterolateral Wall        | I, aVL, V4, V5, V6               | LAD or LCx     | Chest pain, diaphoresis, ECG changes as described in vignette |
| Inferior Wall             | II, III, aVF                     | RCA or LCx     | Often associated with bradycardia, AV block                   |
| Anterior Wall             | V1, V2, V3, V4                   | LAD           |                                                              |
| Apex                      | V1, V2, V3, V4                   | LAD           |                                                              |
| Right Ventricle           | V4R, II, III, aVF                | RCA           | Hypotension, clear lungs, JVD                                |
| Posterior Wall             | ST depression V1-V3, tall R waves | RCA or LCx     |                                                              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The ECG pattern of ST elevation in leads I, aVL, V4, V5, and V6 is characteristic of an anterolateral myocardial infarction. This region is primarily supplied by the Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery, although the high lateral aspect (I, aVL) can sometimes be supplied by the Left Circumflex (LCx) artery depending on coronary dominance. Differentiating MI locations based on ECG leads is crucial for diagnosis and management. Key differentials include inferior, anterior, apical, right ventricular, and posterior MIs, each with distinct ECG patterns.

<a id='question-134'></a>

### Question 134: Myocardial Infarction Territories
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identification of the myocardial territory supplied by the left circumflex artery (LCx) and its clinical correlation with ECG changes and potential complications, specifically lateral wall infarction. (识别左旋支动脉 (LCx) 供血的心肌区域及其与心电图变化和潜在并发症的临床相关性，特别是侧壁梗死。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing chest pain radiating to his left arm, which started 2 hours ago. He is diaphoretic and nauseated. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg and a heart rate of 110 bpm. An initial 12-lead ECG reveals ST-segment elevation in leads I, aVL, V5, and V6, with reciprocal ST-segment depression in leads II, III, and aVF. Troponin levels are markedly elevated. Coronary angiography reveals a complete occlusion of the left circumflex artery (LCx).

Which of the following anatomical structures is MOST likely affected by this myocardial infarction?

(A) Anterior wall of the left ventricle and anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum
(B) Inferior wall of the left ventricle and posterior one-third of the interventricular septum
(C) Lateral wall of the left ventricle and the papillary muscle of the posterior mitral valve leaflet
(D) Inferior wall of the right ventricle and the posterior wall of the right atrium
(E) Anterior wall of the right ventricle and the anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with classic symptoms of acute myocardial infarction (AMI): severe crushing chest pain radiating to the left arm, diaphoresis, nausea.
- **Vital Signs:** Hypotension (90/60 mmHg) and tachycardia (110 bpm) suggest cardiogenic shock or significant myocardial dysfunction.
- **ECG Findings:** ST-segment elevation in leads I, aVL, V5, and V6 is the crucial clue. These leads represent the lateral wall of the left ventricle. Reciprocal ST depression in inferior leads (II, III, aVF) further supports a lateral wall infarction.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated troponin confirms myocardial necrosis.
- **Coronary Angiography:** Occlusion of the left circumflex artery (LCx) identifies the culprit vessel.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical structure most likely affected by the infarction caused by LCx occlusion, given the ECG findings.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Left Circumflex Artery (LCx):** A major branch of the left coronary artery. It typically arises from the left main coronary artery and courses around the left side of the heart in the atrioventricular groove.
- **LCx Supply:** Primarily supplies the left atrium, the lateral and posterior walls of the left ventricle, and often the sinoatrial (SA) node and atrioventricular (AV) node (though this is variable, with the right coronary artery often supplying the AV node).
- **Lateral Wall:** The lateral wall of the left ventricle is supplied by the LCx (and sometimes the diagonal branches of the LAD). ECG leads I, aVL, V5, and V6 specifically monitor the electrical activity of this region.
- **Papillary Muscles:** The mitral valve has two papillary muscles: anterolateral and posteromedial. The anterolateral papillary muscle is typically supplied by the LAD (via diagonal branches), while the posteromedial papillary muscle is typically supplied by the LCx (via obtuse marginal branches).
- **Correlation:** The ECG findings (ST elevation in I, aVL, V5, V6) directly indicate injury to the lateral wall of the left ventricle. Since the LCx is occluded, this artery is the source of the infarction. Therefore, the lateral wall of the left ventricle is affected. The posteromedial papillary muscle, which is often supplied by the LCx, is also located in the posterior/lateral region and can be affected.
- **Option (C) Analysis:** This option correctly identifies the lateral wall of the left ventricle as being supplied by the LCx and implicated by the ECG changes. It also correctly identifies the posteromedial papillary muscle as often supplied by the LCx and located in the posterior/lateral aspect.
- **Option (A) Analysis:** The anterior wall and anterior 2/3 of the septum are typically supplied by the Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery. ECG changes would be seen in leads V1-V4.
- **Option (B) Analysis:** The inferior wall and posterior 1/3 of the septum are typically supplied by the Right Coronary Artery (RCA) or the LCx (in left-dominant circulation). ECG changes would be seen in leads II, III, aVF, and sometimes V1-V3 (posterior leads).
- **Option (D) Analysis:** The inferior wall of the right ventricle and posterior wall of the right atrium are typically supplied by the RCA.
- **Option (E) Analysis:** The anterior wall of the right ventricle and anterior 2/3 of the septum are typically supplied by the LAD.

##### 3. Embryological Considerations (胚胎发生学考点)
- The heart develops from the primitive heart tube, which loops and septates. The different chambers and walls arise from specific regions. The lateral walls of the ventricles are formed from bulbi arteriosi and bulbi ventriculi. The coronary arteries arise from the aortic arches and undergo complex remodeling. The specific territories supplied by the LAD, LCx, and RCA are largely determined by the developmental pathways of these vessels and the structures they perfuse.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. The anterior wall of the left ventricle and the anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum are primarily supplied by the Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery. ST elevation in these areas would typically be seen in leads V1-V4.
- (B): Incorrect. The inferior wall of the left ventricle and the posterior one-third of the interventricular septum are primarily supplied by the Right Coronary Artery (RCA) in right-dominant circulation (most common) or the LCx in left-dominant circulation. ST elevation would typically be seen in leads II, III, aVF, and potentially V1-V3 (posterior leads).
- (D): Incorrect. The inferior wall of the right ventricle and the posterior wall of the right atrium are primarily supplied by the Right Coronary Artery (RCA).
- (E): Incorrect. The anterior wall of the right ventricle and the anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum are primarily supplied by the Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure/Territory                  | Artery Supply (Typical) | ECG Leads Affected (ST Elevation) |
| :----------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| Anterior LV Wall & Ant 2/3 Septum    | LAD                    | V1-V4                             |
| Inferior LV Wall & Post 1/3 Septum    | RCA (or LCx)            | II, III, aVF, V1-V3 (posterior)    |
| Lateral LV Wall                      | LCx                    | I, aVL, V5, V6                     |
| Right Ventricle (Inferior & Post)    | RCA                    | V4R (Right-sided leads)           |
| Right Ventricle (Anterior & Ant 2/3 Septum) | LAD                    | V1-V4                             |
| Anterolateral Papillary Muscle       | LAD                    | V3-V6                             |
| Posteromedial Papillary Muscle       | LCx (or RCA)            | V5-V6, I, aVL                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The ECG findings (ST elevation in I, aVL, V5, V6) are pathognomonic for a lateral wall myocardial infarction. The left circumflex artery (LCx) is the primary blood supply to the lateral wall of the left ventricle and often supplies the posteromedial papillary muscle. Occlusion of the LCx leads to infarction in these regions. Differentiating MI territories based on ECG leads is crucial for diagnosis and management. Key differentials include LAD (anterior/septal) and RCA (inferior/posterior/RV) territories.

<a id='question-135'></a>

### Question 135: MI Territories: Inferior Wall (RCA / PDA: II, III, aVF)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Inferior myocardial infarction (MI) typically results from occlusion of the right coronary artery (RCA) or the posterior descending artery (PDA), leading to ECG changes in leads II, III, and aVF, and potentially right ventricular involvement. (下壁心肌梗死通常由右冠状动脉 (RCA) 或其主要分支——右位向后下行支 (RPDA) 闭塞引起，导致 II、III、aVF 导联的 ECG 改变，并可能累及右心室。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing substernal chest pain radiating to his right arm, which started 2 hours ago. He is diaphoretic and nauseated. His vital signs are: BP 90/60 mmHg, HR 110 bpm, RR 24/min, SpO2 92% on room air. Physical examination reveals cool, clammy skin and distant heart sounds. An emergent 12-lead ECG shows ST-segment elevation in leads II, III, and aVF, along with reciprocal ST-segment depression in leads I and aVL. Troponin levels are markedly elevated. Given the ECG findings indicative of an inferior wall MI, which of the following structures is most likely affected by the occlusion?

(A) Left anterior descending artery (LAD) supplying the anterior wall and septum
(B) Circumflex artery (LCx) supplying the lateral wall
(C) Right coronary artery (RCA) supplying the inferior wall, right ventricle, and SA/AV nodes
(D) Left main coronary artery (LMCA) supplying the LAD and LCx
(E) Posterior descending artery (PDA) arising from the RCA, supplying the inferior wall and posterior septum

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, risk factors (HTN, hyperlipidemia), acute crushing chest pain radiating to the right arm, diaphoresis, nausea. Classic presentation for acute myocardial infarction (AMI).
- **Vitals:** Hypotension (BP 90/60), tachycardia (HR 110), tachypnea (RR 24), hypoxia (SpO2 92%). Suggests cardiogenic shock or significant hemodynamic compromise due to MI.
- **ECG Findings:** ST-segment elevation in leads II, III, and aVF. This pattern is pathognomonic for an inferior wall MI. Reciprocal ST depression in I and aVL further supports this localization.
- **Labs:** Elevated troponin confirms myocardial necrosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is most likely affected by the occlusion causing the inferior wall MI.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Inferior Wall MI:** The inferior wall of the left ventricle is primarily supplied by the posterior descending artery (PDA).
- **Coronary Artery Anatomy:**
    - The **Right Coronary Artery (RCA)** originates from the right aortic sinus. In approximately 85% of individuals (right-dominant circulation), the RCA gives rise to the PDA, which then supplies the inferior wall, the posterior third of the interventricular septum, and often the posterior wall of the left ventricle. The RCA also supplies the right ventricle and the sinoatrial (SA) and atrioventricular (AV) nodes.
    - The **Left Circumflex Artery (LCx)** originates from the left aortic sinus. In approximately 15% of individuals (left-dominant circulation), the LCx gives rise to the PDA.
    - The **Left Anterior Descending Artery (LAD)** originates from the left aortic sinus and supplies the anterior wall, apex, and anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum.
    - The **Left Main Coronary Artery (LMCA)** bifurcates into the LAD and LCx.
- **Clinical Correlation:** ST elevation in leads II, III, and aVF indicates injury to the inferior wall. Since the RCA is the most common source of the PDA (and thus the inferior wall supply), occlusion of the RCA is the most frequent cause of inferior wall MI. Occlusion of the PDA itself (if arising from RCA or LCx) would also cause this pattern. However, the RCA itself supplies the inferior wall directly in many cases, and its occlusion often affects the PDA territory. Given the options, the RCA is the most likely culprit vessel supplying the inferior wall territory indicated by the ECG. Option (E) is also correct anatomically regarding the PDA supplying the inferior wall, but the RCA (Option C) is the *source* of the PDA in most people and also directly supplies parts of the inferior wall and the right ventricle, making it the most comprehensive answer for the *affected structure* leading to this ECG pattern. The question asks which *structure* is most likely affected, and the RCA is the major vessel whose occlusion typically causes this.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Left anterior descending artery (LAD)** supplies the anterior wall (V1-V4) and anterior septum (V1-V2). Occlusion causes ST elevation in anterior leads (V1-V4), not inferior leads (II, III, aVF). Incorrect.
- (B): **Circumflex artery (LCx)** supplies the lateral wall (I, aVL, V5, V6) and sometimes the posterior wall. Occlusion causes ST elevation in lateral leads (I, aVL, V5, V6) or posterior leads (ST depression in V1-V3, tall R waves in V1-V2). Incorrect.
- (D): **Left main coronary artery (LMCA)** occlusion is catastrophic, causing extensive anterior, lateral, and septal MI. It would affect both LAD and LCx territories, leading to widespread ST elevation (anterior, lateral) and depression (inferior, posterior). This presentation is inconsistent with isolated inferior ST elevation. Incorrect.
- (E): **Posterior descending artery (PDA)** supplies the inferior wall and posterior septum. Occlusion *does* cause inferior MI (ST elevation in II, III, aVF). However, the PDA usually arises from the RCA (right dominance). Therefore, occlusion of the RCA is the *most common* cause of PDA occlusion and inferior MI. While technically correct that the PDA territory is affected, the RCA is the primary vessel whose occlusion leads to this specific clinical picture in the majority of cases. Option (C) is a more encompassing and statistically likely answer representing the affected *structure* (the major artery).

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Territory Supplied                                  | ECG Leads (ST Elevation) | Origin (Typical) | Clinical Significance                                                                 |
| :------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :--------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **RCA (Correct Answer)**  | Inferior wall, RV, SA/AV nodes, Posterior septum   | II, III, aVF, V1 (RV)     | Right Aortic Sinus | Most common cause of inferior MI, RV MI, bradyarrhythmias (SA/AV node ischemia)      |
| LAD (Distractor A)        | Anterior wall, Apex, Anterior 2/3 Septum            | V1-V4                   | Left Aortic Sinus | Most common cause of anterior MI, often leads to cardiogenic shock, arrhythmias      |
| LCx (Distractor B)        | Lateral wall, Posterior wall (sometimes)            | I, aVL, V5, V6, V7-V9    | Left Aortic Sinus | Cause of lateral/posterior MI, can cause AV block if proximal                          |
| LMCA (Distractor D)       | LAD & LCx territories                               | Widespread (Anterior, Lateral) | Left Aortic Sinus | Catastrophic MI, high mortality                                                       |
| PDA (Distractor E)        | Inferior wall, Posterior 1/3 Septum                 | II, III, aVF             | RCA (usually)    | Supplies the inferior wall; occlusion causes inferior MI. Often occluded *with* RCA. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Inferior wall MI (ST elevation in II, III, aVF) is most commonly caused by occlusion of the RCA, which supplies the inferior wall via the PDA (in right-dominant circulation) and also the right ventricle and conduction system. Differentiating MI territories based on ECG leads is crucial for diagnosis and management. Remember that the RCA is the most common culprit for inferior MI.

<a id='question-136'></a>

### Question 136: MI Territories: Posterior Wall (PDA / LCx: ST Depression V1-V3 with Tall R Waves)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the electrocardiographic (ECG) findings associated with posterior myocardial infarction (MI), specifically reciprocal changes in anterior leads (ST depression in V1-V3) and tall R waves in V1-V2, and correlating these with the anatomical location of the infarct (posterior wall supplied by the posterior descending artery, PDA).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing substernal chest pain that began 2 hours ago and radiates to his left arm and jaw. He is diaphoretic and nauseated. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg and a heart rate of 110 bpm. Initial ECG shows sinus tachycardia. A 12-lead ECG performed 30 minutes later reveals ST-segment depression in leads V1, V2, and V3, along with prominent R waves in leads V1 and V2. There is no ST elevation in the standard limb or precordial leads. Troponin levels are elevated.

Which coronary artery is most likely occluded, leading to this ECG pattern and clinical presentation?

(A) Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery
(B) Left Circumflex (LCx) artery
(C) Right Coronary Artery (RCA)
(D) Left Main Coronary Artery
(E) Posterior Descending Artery (PDA)

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, risk factors (HTN, hyperlipidemia), acute severe chest pain radiating to arm/jaw, diaphoresis, nausea, hypotension, tachycardia – classic presentation for acute myocardial infarction (MI).
- **ECG Findings:** ST depression in V1-V3 and tall R waves in V1-V2. This specific pattern is highly suggestive of a posterior wall MI. ST depression in V1-V3 represents reciprocal changes to ST elevation that would be seen in posterior leads (V7-V9) if they were recorded. Tall R waves in V1-V2 occur because the electrical forces from the infarcted posterior wall are no longer contributing to the normal anterior forces, making the anterior forces appear relatively larger.
- **Troponin Levels:** Elevated troponin confirms myocardial necrosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific coronary artery occlusion responsible for this clinical and ECG picture.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Posterior Wall Anatomy:** The posterior wall of the left ventricle is supplied primarily by the Posterior Descending Artery (PDA).
- **PDA Origin:** The origin of the PDA determines coronary dominance. In about 85% of individuals (right dominance), the PDA arises from the Right Coronary Artery (RCA). In about 15% (left dominance), it arises from the Left Circumflex (LCx) artery. In a small percentage, it arises from both (co-dominance).
- **ECG Correlation:** An occlusion of the PDA leads to infarction of the posterior wall. This causes ST elevation in the posterior leads (V7-V9, if recorded). Since these leads are not standard, the reciprocal changes are seen in the anterior leads (V1-V3), manifesting as ST depression. The loss of electrical activity from the posterior wall also leads to increased amplitude of the R wave in the anterior leads (V1-V2).
- **Differential Diagnosis:**
    - **LAD occlusion:** Typically causes ST elevation in V1-V4 (anteroseptal/anterior MI) or V2-V6 (anterolateral MI).
    - **LCx occlusion:** Typically causes ST elevation in I, aVL, V5, V6 (lateral MI). If the LCx gives rise to the PDA (left dominance), it can cause posterior MI.
    - **RCA occlusion:** Typically causes ST elevation in II, III, aVF (inferior MI). If the RCA gives rise to the PDA (right dominance), it can cause posterior MI.
    - **Left Main occlusion:** Usually causes widespread ST elevation (anterior, lateral, inferior) and profound hemodynamic instability.
- **Conclusion:** The ECG findings (ST depression V1-V3, tall R waves V1-V2) strongly point to a posterior wall MI. The artery supplying the posterior wall is the PDA. Therefore, occlusion of the PDA is the most likely cause.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery:** Occlusion of the LAD typically causes anterior or anteroseptal MI, characterized by ST elevation in leads V1-V4. This is inconsistent with the ST depression in V1-V3 seen in the vignette.
- (B) **Left Circumflex (LCx) artery:** Occlusion of the LCx typically causes lateral MI (ST elevation in I, aVL, V5, V6). While the LCx can supply the PDA in left-dominant circulation, leading to posterior MI, the question asks for the *specific artery* supplying the infarcted region (posterior wall), which is the PDA itself. Furthermore, isolated LCx occlusion without PDA involvement usually doesn't cause this specific ECG pattern.
- (C) **Right Coronary Artery (RCA):** Occlusion of the RCA typically causes inferior MI (ST elevation in II, III, aVF). Similar to the LCx, the RCA can supply the PDA in right-dominant circulation, leading to posterior MI. However, the question asks for the artery directly supplying the posterior wall, which is the PDA. Also, isolated RCA occlusion usually presents with inferior MI changes.
- (D) **Left Main Coronary Artery:** Occlusion of the left main artery is a catastrophic event, usually causing extensive anterior, lateral, and sometimes inferior MI, leading to widespread ST elevation and severe hemodynamic compromise. The ECG pattern described is specific for posterior MI, not a left main occlusion.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | LAD Occlusion          | LCx Occlusion          | RCA Occlusion          | PDA Occlusion          | Left Main Occlusion     |
|----------------------|------------------------|------------------------|------------------------|------------------------|------------------------|
| **Typical MI Area** | Anterior/Anteroseptal  | Lateral                | Inferior               | Posterior              | Extensive (Anterior, Lateral, Inferior) |
| **ECG ST Elevation** | V1-V4                  | I, aVL, V5, V6         | II, III, aVF           | V7-V9 (reciprocal ST depression V1-V3) | Widespread ST elevation |
| **ECG ST Depression**| Reciprocal in inferior | Reciprocal in inferior | Reciprocal in anterior | V1-V3 (tall R waves V1-V2) | N/A                    |
| **Artery Occluded** | LAD                    | LCx                    | RCA                    | PDA                    | Left Main              |
| **Dominance**        | N/A                    | N/A                    | N/A                    | Determines RCA/LCx origin | N/A                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The classic ECG findings of ST depression in V1-V3 accompanied by tall R waves in V1-V2 are highly specific for posterior wall myocardial infarction. This area is supplied by the Posterior Descending Artery (PDA). Remember to consider coronary dominance (RCA vs. LCx origin of PDA) when localizing the infarct, but the question asks for the artery supplying the affected wall itself. Differentiate this pattern from anterior MI (LAD), lateral MI (LCx), inferior MI (RCA), and left main occlusion (widespread changes).

<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: MI Territories: Right Ventricular Infarct (Proximal RCA: ST Elevation V4R, Nitroglycerin Contraindication)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical distribution of the right coronary artery (RCA) and its branches, specifically the proximal RCA supplying the right ventricle, and the clinical implications of occlusion, including ECG changes (ST elevation in V4R) and contraindications to certain therapies (nitroglycerin due to preload dependence).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of acute, severe substernal chest pain radiating to his right arm, accompanied by diaphoresis and nausea. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 85/50 mmHg, heart rate of 110 bpm, respiratory rate of 24 breaths/min, and oxygen saturation of 92% on room air. Physical examination reveals cool, clammy skin and jugular venous distension (JVD). An initial 12-lead ECG shows ST-segment elevation in leads II, III, aVF, and V4R. Serial cardiac enzymes are elevated. The patient is started on aspirin, clopidogrel, and heparin. He is given morphine for pain relief. However, upon administration of sublingual nitroglycerin, his blood pressure drops further to 70/40 mmHg, and he becomes increasingly hypotensive and diaphoretic.

Which of the following anatomical structures is most likely responsible for the patient's hemodynamic instability and contraindication to nitroglycerin?

(A) Left anterior descending artery (LAD) supplying the anterior wall of the left ventricle and anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum.
(B) Circumflex artery (LCx) supplying the lateral wall of the left ventricle and posterior one-third of the interventricular septum.
(C) Proximal right coronary artery (RCA) supplying the right atrium, right ventricle, sinoatrial (SA) node (in most individuals), and atrioventricular (AV) node.
(D) Left main coronary artery (LMCA) supplying both the LAD and LCx.
(E) Posterior descending artery (PDA) arising from the LCx (left dominant circulation) supplying the inferior wall of the left ventricle and posterior one-third of the interventricular septum.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62M, HTN, HLD, acute severe chest pain radiating to right arm, diaphoresis, nausea. Classic presentation for acute myocardial infarction (MI).
- **Vitals:** Hypotension (BP 85/50 -> 70/40), tachycardia (HR 110), tachypnea (RR 24), hypoxia (SpO2 92%). Suggests cardiogenic shock or significant hemodynamic compromise.
- **Physical Exam:** Cool, clammy skin (poor perfusion), JVD (elevated right atrial pressure, suggesting right ventricular dysfunction or failure).
- **ECG:** ST elevation in II, III, aVF (inferior wall MI) AND V4R (right ventricular MI). This combination is highly suggestive of a proximal RCA occlusion.
- **Labs:** Elevated cardiac enzymes confirm MI.
- **Treatment Response:** Hypotension worsens significantly after nitroglycerin administration. Nitroglycerin is a vasodilator that reduces preload and afterload. In patients with right ventricular infarction, the right ventricle is preload-dependent to maintain cardiac output. Reducing preload with nitroglycerin can lead to severe hypotension and cardiogenic shock.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical structure responsible for the hemodynamic instability and nitroglycerin contraindication.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Right Coronary Artery (RCA) Anatomy:** The RCA typically arises from the right aortic sinus. It travels in the right atrioventricular groove, supplying the right atrium and right ventricle. Crucially, it often gives rise to the posterior descending artery (PDA) and the posterior left ventricular artery (PLVA) in right-dominant circulation (approx. 85% of people). The proximal RCA supplies the SA node (in ~60% of people) and the AV node (in ~90% of people).
- **Right Ventricular Infarction (RV Infarct):** Occlusion of the proximal RCA leads to infarction of the right ventricle, often accompanied by inferior wall infarction of the left ventricle (due to PDA involvement). RV infarction impairs the right ventricle's ability to pump blood into the pulmonary circulation, leading to decreased left ventricular preload and subsequent hypotension. The right ventricle is particularly preload-dependent.
- **ECG Findings:** ST elevation in right-sided leads (V3R, V4R) is characteristic of RV infarction. ST elevation in inferior leads (II, III, aVF) indicates inferior wall involvement, often seen with proximal RCA occlusion.
- **Nitroglycerin Contraindication:** Nitroglycerin reduces preload by causing venodilation. In RV infarction, the left ventricle relies heavily on the compromised right ventricle for adequate filling. Reducing preload further with nitroglycerin can precipitate severe hypotension and cardiogenic shock.
- **Other Options:**
    - (A) LAD supplies the anterior LV and septum. Occlusion causes anterior/septal MI, typically presenting with ST elevation in V1-V4 (anterior) or V1-V3 (septal). Does not typically cause RV infarction or JVD/preload dependence.
    - (B) LCx supplies the lateral/posterior LV. Occlusion causes lateral/posterior MI, typically presenting with ST elevation in I, aVL, V5-V6 (lateral) or ST depression in V1-V3 with tall R waves (posterior). Does not typically cause RV infarction or JVD/preload dependence.
    - (D) LMCA supplies both LAD and LCx. Occlusion is usually catastrophic, causing extensive anterior, lateral, and septal infarction. Does not specifically target the RV.
    - (E) PDA (from LCx in left dominance) supplies the inferior LV and posterior septum. While occlusion can cause inferior MI (ST elevation in II, III, aVF), it does not typically cause RV infarction or the specific hemodynamic profile seen here unless the LCx occlusion is very proximal and also affects RV branches. The ECG finding in V4R strongly points to RCA involvement.

##### 3. Embryological Considerations (胚胎发生学考点)
- The coronary arteries develop from endothelial progenitor cells surrounding the developing heart tube. The RCA and LCA (which bifurcates into LAD and LCx) arise from the truncus arteriosus. The specific branching patterns and dominance (right vs. left) are established during development. The RCA's supply to the SA and AV nodes is crucial for cardiac rhythm, and its supply to the RV is critical for right-sided cardiac function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The LAD supplies the anterior LV and septum. An LAD occlusion would cause anterior/septal MI, typically presenting with ST elevation in leads V1-V4. This patient has inferior (II, III, aVF) and right ventricular (V4R) ST elevation, inconsistent with an LAD occlusion. Furthermore, LAD occlusion does not typically cause the severe preload dependence seen in RV infarction.
- (B): The LCx supplies the lateral and posterior LV. An LCx occlusion would cause lateral/posterior MI, typically presenting with ST elevation in leads I, aVL, V5-V6 or ST depression in V1-V3. This patient's ECG findings (II, III, aVF, V4R) are inconsistent with an LCx occlusion. LCx occlusion does not typically cause RV infarction or severe preload dependence.
- (D): The LMCA supplies both the LAD and LCx. Occlusion is usually devastating, causing massive anterior, lateral, and septal infarction. While it could potentially affect the RCA ostium in some rare anatomical variations, the classic presentation here (inferior + RV MI) points specifically to the RCA territory. LMCA occlusion typically presents with widespread ST elevation (V1-V6, I, aVL) and profound hemodynamic instability, but not specifically the preload dependence characteristic of RV infarction.
- (E): The PDA supplies the inferior LV wall and posterior septum. In left-dominant circulation (15% of people), the PDA arises from the LCx. Occlusion of the PDA (from LCx) would cause inferior MI (ST elevation in II, III, aVF) but typically *not* RV infarction (V4R elevation) unless the LCx occlusion is very proximal. The combination of inferior and RV ST elevation strongly implicates the RCA, which usually supplies the PDA in right-dominant circulation. Even if the PDA came from the LCx, the RV involvement points away from this option.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Proximal RCA Occlusion (Correct) | LAD Occlusion (A) | LCx Occlusion (B) | LMCA Occlusion (D) | PDA Occlusion (E) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|--------------------|--------------------|---------------------|--------------------|
| **Territory**        | RV, Inferior LV (often), SA/AV Node | Anterior LV, Septum | Lateral/Posterior LV | Anterior, Lateral, Septum | Inferior LV, Post Septum |
| **Typical ECG**      | ST elevation II, III, aVF, V4R     | ST elevation V1-V4 | ST elevation I, aVL, V5-V6 | Widespread ST elevation | ST elevation II, III, aVF |
| **RV Involvement**   | Yes (Common)                      | No                 | No                 | No                  | No                 |
| **JVD**              | Yes (Common)                      | No                 | No                 | No                  | No                 |
| **Preload Dependence** | Yes (Significant)                 | No                 | No                 | No                  | No                 |
| **Nitroglycerin Contraindication** | Yes (Strong)                 | No                 | No                 | No                  | No                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Right ventricular infarction, typically caused by proximal RCA occlusion, leads to right ventricular failure and preload dependence. This manifests clinically as hypotension, JVD, and a contraindication to preload-reducing agents like nitroglycerin. ECG findings often include ST elevation in right-sided leads (V4R) in addition to inferior leads (II, III, aVF). Differentiating RV infarct from isolated inferior LV infarct is crucial for appropriate management.
